## Page 1


Convergence rate estimates for semigroups and heat kernels
associated with resistance forms
Koyo Oishi∗
Abstract
In this paper, we derive quantitative convergence rates for stochastic processes associated with
resistance forms. While the qualitative convergence of heat kernels and semigroups under the Gromov-
Hausdorff-vague convergence of underlying measured resistance metric spaces has been investigated
previously, their quantitative convergence rates have remained unexplored.
We establish explicit
convergence rates for the associated semigroups and heat kernels under the assumptions of measure
regularity and lower resistance estimates. Furthermore, we introduce a new metric that induces the
Gromov-Hausdorff-vague topology, and is convenient for evaluation.
As applications of our main
results, we present two illustrative examples.
First, we derive first estimate on the convergence
rate for the random walk approximation of Brownian motion on the Sierpinski gasket. Second, we
apply our results to the one-dimensional Bouchaud trap model, successfully extending the previously
known parameter regime to all cases where homogenization occurs and improving the convergence
rate estimates in the existing regime by at least a quadratic factor.

Introduction
In the study of limit theorems for stochastic processes, once convergence is established, obtaining a
corresponding rate is a natural and important problem from both theoretical and applied perspectives. For
instance, the Berry-Esseen theorem [12, 26], which provides a rate estimate for the central limit theorem,
stands as a monumental achievement in probability theory. Regarding the assessment of convergence rates
for i.i.d. sums, the Kolmós-Major-Tusnády approximation shown in [42, 43] is also widely recognized.
While these represent studies on the convergence rate for sums of i.i.d. random variables, research has
also been conducted on the convergence of more complex spaces and the associated random walks, as
well as their convergence rates. Examples are shown below:
• In [10, 32, 45], Brownian motion on the Sierpinski gasket was constructed as a limit of the random
walk on the finite approximating graphs. Rate estimates for the associated generators and resolvents
were obtained in [57].
• Convergence of a process called slow bond random walk and a rate estimate were established in
[25].
• In [41], a convergence rate estimate for a continuous time random walk converging to a fractional
diffusion was provided.
One of the powerful frameworks for dealing with the stochastic processes on complex, fractal-like is
the theory of resistance forms. We provide a brief introduction to resistance forms here, referring the
reader to Section 2.3 for a more details. Recall that a resistance form on a set F, as introduced by Kigami
[38, 39], is a pair (E, F) consisting of a bilinear form E and its domain F (a function space on F). It is
well known that a space F equipped with a resistance form is naturally endowed with a metric known as
the resistance metric (see Definition 2.33 and Theorem 2.35). Furthermore, if F is regular in the sense of
Definition 2.36, any full-support Radon measure µ on F induces a corresponding regular Dirichlet form
(E, D) on L2(F, µ). Consequently, a Hunt process can be naturally constructed on any regular resistance
metric space equipped with a fully supported Radon measure.
Convergence results of the stochastic processes associated with resistance forms have been well inves-
tigated. For instance:
• In [18], it was shown that the rescaled random walk on a random graph satisfying suitable assump-
tions converges to the Brownian motion on the random tree whose search-depth function is the
Brownian excursion.
• It was shown in [6] that if a sequence of measured metric trees (Tn, νn) converges to a limiting
measured metric tree (T, ν) in an appropriate sense (under certain additional assumptions), then
the processes with speed measures νn on Tn converge to the process with speed measure ν on T.
• Following recent advancements, including [4, 52], the scaling limit of the random walk on critical
percolation on the triangular lattice has been identified. In this paper, the theory of resistance
forms is also utilized.
• Also, there are general convergence results. In [19, 21, 56], the convergence of the process on a
measured resistance metric space and its local time were demonstrated.
Given the correspondence between measured resistance metric spaces and stochastic processes, the con-
tinuity of this correspondence, namely the convergence of the corresponding probabilistic objects in a
suitable sense, has been extensively studied.
For the Sierpinski gasket, which is a fundamental ex-
ample of a resistance metric space, Goldstein originally constructed Brownian motion via finite graph
approximations. For the Sierpinski gasket, which is a fundamental example of a resistance metric space,
Goldstein’s construction of Brownian motion via finite graph approximations serves as a classical exam-
ple of such convergence. In recent years, further qualitative results have been established. Using the
Gromov-Hausdorff-vague topology, several answers to this question have been provided in recent years.
As mentioned above, Croydon, Hambly, and Kumagai [19] established the convergence of the processes
2


## Page 3


Convergence rate estimates for semigroups and heat kernels associated with resistance forms
and their local times under the uniform volume doubling (UVD) condition. Relaxing the UVD condition,
Croydon [21] proved the convergence of the stochastic processes under a non-explosion condition, while
Noda [56] demonstrated the convergence of the local times under a metric entropy condition combined
with the non-explosion condition. So far, none of these results were quantitative, and providing such
results is the goal of this article.
In research on stochastic processes on random spaces and fractals, including the aforementioned
studies, the Gromov-Hausdorff type topology now serves as a standard framework. Notable examples
of research that established the foundation for applying the GH-type topology to probability theory
are the work of [1, 28, 51].
Subsequently, the framework for the convergence of metric spaces with
additional structures, including the Gromov-Hausdorff-vague topology, was studied and underwent major
development in [36, 54].
This study also employs the theory of Gromov-Hausdorff-type topologies established in these prior
works. However, the Prokhorov metric, which is commonly used to define the Gromov-Hausdorff-vague
topology, does not appear to capture the information necessary for the quantitative estimates that are
the goal of this paper. Therefore, we first define a distance between finite measures on a metric space
(M, dM), which is expected to be better suited for quantitative evaluation, as follows:

Remark 1.4. Although these estimates are likely not optimal, it is novel that the convergence rate has
been proven to be at least polynomial with respect to the distance of the space.
Remark 1.5. One can find the proof of Theorem 1.2(i) in Corollary 6.3, and those of Theorem 1.2(ii)
and (iii) in Theorem 5.1. Also, Theorem 1.3 is shown in Theorem 6.1.
Remark 1.6. Theorem 1.2 is established via an argument based on estimates for resolvents. On the
other hand, an alternative estimate for semigroups can be obtained using heat kernel estimates (see
Corollary 6.3). However, since this approach generally yields a suboptimal convergence rate compared to
the present theorem (see Remark 6.4), we omit a detailed description of the latter estimate here.

The proof of the main theorem proceeds in the following steps. First, we establish error estimates for
the resolvent (see Theorem 4.1). The idea for this part is largely inspired by [21], relying on the fact that
the Green function of the process killed at a single point can be expressed using the resistance metric,
and that the α-resolvent of the original process can be written in terms of the associated Green operator.
Next, using these resolvent estimates, we derive error estimates for the semigroup acting on test func-
tions belonging to the domain of the generator (more precisely, the domain of its square; see Lemma 5.6).
To approximate a general bounded Hölder continuous function f by functions in the domain, we consider
the action of a short-time semigroup on f, which provides the necessary smoothing to obtain the desired
semigroup estimates. For the heat kernel, the basic idea is fundamentally the same: we approximate the
Dirac measure (an approximate identity) by acting a short-time semigroup on the indicator function of
a small open ball.
Regarding the non-compact case, we obtain the results by approximating the space by an increas-
ing sequence of compact sets. Assumption 1.8 ensures that this approximating sequence satisfies the
conditions required for the compact case.
To carry out these quantitative estimates rigorously, precise Hölder continuity estimates for the heat
kernel, Ptf, and the α-potential density are indispensable. The analysis related to these quantities is
conducted in Section 3. In the process, we also prove convergence of hitting times (see Theorem 3.9), a
result we believe to be of independent interest for various applications.
As applications of our results, we present two illustrative examples in this paper: the Sierpinski gasket
(SG) and the Bouchaud trap model (BTM).
The Sierpinski gasket is one of the most well-investigated self-similar fractals (see Figure 1 below).
While the convergence of random walks on approximating graphs to Brownian motion on the gasket is
now a well-known fact [10, 32, 45], this paper is the first to rigorously derive an estimate on its quantitative
convergence rate. As an immediate consequence of our results, we may confirm that

Figure 1: Sierpinski Gasket
Remark 1.15. We used the open set condition and the fact that the resistance metric and the Euclidean
metric are comparable up to a power to verify Assumption 1.1 for the Sierpinski gasket. Note that (A2)
or (A3) are satisfied provided that the connectivity of the space or the continuity of the process is
established. Consequently, our results apply to a wider class of fractals satisfying these properties, such
as nested fractals. We also conjecture that our results are applicable to the two-dimensional Sierpinski
carpet under some modifications.
BTM is a model of aging, which refers to the phenomenon in which a system’s dynamics progres-
sively slow down without reaching equilibrium on laboratory time scales, so that its current state carries
information about its age. A rough description of the model is as follows: First, for given α > 0, we
introduce i.i.d. weights (τi)i∈Z with P(τ0 > u) = u−α, u > 1 and construct a measure µn on n−1Z by
µn = 1
n
P
i∈Z τiδi/n. We regard (n−1Z, µn) equipped with the usual Euclidean distance dE as a measured
resistance metric space, and investigate the scaling limit of the space and the corresponding process
Xn = (Xn
t ). Note that, if α > 1, (n−1Z, dE, µn) converges to (R, dE, E[τ0]dx) in the Gromov-Hausdorff-
vague topology almost surely, and the limit process X = (Xt)t of Xn is a Brownian motion scaled by a
deterministic factor.
Quantitative estimates for the speed of convergence for the distribution function and the heat kernel
were shown in [3]. Since they used an arguments involving second moment, the regime of α is restricted to
α > 2. As an application of our results, we extend the regime of α to α > 1 in Theorem 8.14. Moreover,
by virtue of a near-optimal estimate for the BLκ distance between µn and the limiting measure, our result
significantly improves the previous estimates from [3], at least doubling the exponent of the convergence
rate. Figure 2 compares the estimates for the distribution function in [3] and in this paper. Although
the range of α is somewhat restricted, we also obtain annealed estimates for the semigroup and the heat
kernel, which were unknown. See Theorems 8.15, 8.23 and 8.24 for more details.
Remark 1.16. Our main result is also applicable to the setting considered in [25]. However, since the
resulting estimates were weaker than those in [25, Theorem 2.4], we omit the details in this paper.
The organization of this paper is as follows. In Section 2, we introduce the metric defined in (1.1)
and the corresponding Gromov-Hausdorff-type distance, along with the concept of resistance forms and
related known results. In Section 3, we investigate the Hölder continuity of the heat kernel, Ptf for
suitable functions f, and the α-potential density. Sections 4 to 6 are devoted to establishing the resolvent
estimates, the semigroup estimates, and the heat kernel estimates, respectively. In Section 7, we consider
the non-compact case. Finally, in Section 8, we present examples illustrating the applications of our
results.
Lastly, we make some remarks on notation used in this paper.
• We write a ∧b := min{a, b} and a ∨b := max{a, b} for a, b ∈R ∪{±∞}.
• We also note that throughout the paper, a constant appearing in proposition, lemma, or theorem
X is denoted by cX.

Preliminaries
2.1
Bounded Lipschitz metric
In this section, we introduce an appropriate metric on a space of measures. In [21], the Gromov-Hausdorff-
vague metric (or topology) is used to formulate the convergence of root-and-measured metric spaces.
Although the metric also works in our setting, it is unsuitable for our purpose. More precisely, we aim
to establish estimates for semigroups and heat kernels using the distance between spaces. To acheive
this, roughly speaking, we need to estimate the difference |
R
fdµ −
R
fdν| by using the metric between
µ and ν. However, the (suitably adapted to non-compact settings) Prokhorov metric, which is used to
define the Gromov-Hausdorff-vague metric, does not seem to capture the necessary information for this.
Therefore, we first need to define an alternative metric that is better suited to this, and that is the aim
of this subsection.
In this paper, for a metric space (X, dX), x ∈X, and r > 0, we define BdX(x, r) and DdX(x, r) as
follows.
BdX(x, r) := {x ∈X : dX(x, y) < r}, and DdX(x, r) := {y ∈X : dX(x, y) ≤r}.
If the metric dX is clear in the context, we omit dX from the notation.
Definition 2.1. Let (X, dX) be a metric space. For a function f : X →R, a non-empty subset A ⊆X
and a real number κ ∈(0, 1], we define ∥f∥A, ∥f∥Lipκ(A) ∈[0, ∞], and ∥f∥BLκ(A) ∈[0, ∞] as follows.
∥f∥A := sup
a∈A
|f(a)|,
∥f∥Lipκ(A) := sup
x,y∈A
x̸=y
|f(x) −f(y)|
dX(x, y)κ
,
and
∥f∥BLκ(A) := ∥f∥A + ∥f∥Lipκ(A).
If A is a singleton, we set ∥f∥Lipκ(A) = 0. The quantity ∥f∥Lipκ(A) is the κ-Hölder constant of f|A, and
we call ∥· ∥BLκ(A) the BLκ(A) norm or simply the BLκ norm if A is clear from the context. Also, we
denote the set of functions with a finite BLκ(A) norm by BLκ(A). Namely,
BLκ(A) := {f : X →R : ∥f∥BLκ(A) < ∞}.

The following lemma plays an important role, and is known as the McShane’s extension theorem. The
lemma is well known, but we provide a proof for the reader’s convenience.
Lemma 2.4 ([50, Theorem 1]). Let A be a non-empty subset of a metric space (X, dX).
For any
f : A →R with ∥f∥BLκ(A) < ∞, there exists an extension ¯f : X →R such that ∥¯f∥X = ∥f∥A and
∥¯f∥Lipκ(X) = ∥f∥Lipκ(A).
Remark 2.5. Strictly speaking, it would be more precise to denote the above extension, for instance, by
¯f A rather than ¯f, since, for example, the extensions of 1[0,1] : [0, 1] →R and 1[0,1] : [−2, −1] ∪[0, 1] →R
may differ. Here, we denote the indicator function of A by 1A for a set X and its subset A. Nevertheless,
in what follows, we omit the superscript A from the notation, as no confusion will arise.
Proof. Once we construct an extension g : X →R of f satisfying ∥g∥Lipκ(X) = ∥f∥Lipκ(A), it is easy to
obtain the desired extension ¯f. In fact, it is enough to set ¯f := ((sup f(A)) ∧g) ∨inf f(A). Thus, we
focus on showing the existence of such a function g.
Let L := ∥f∥Lipκ(A) ∈[0, ∞) and define g : X →(−∞, ∞] by setting

Definition 2.6 (McShane extension). Let A be a non-empty subset of a metric space (X, dX) and f
be an element in BLκ(A). We say a function ¯f : X →R is a McShane extension of f : A →R (with
exponent κ) if ¯f satisfies ¯f|A = f, ∥¯f∥X = ∥f∥A, and ∥¯f∥Lipκ(X) = ∥f∥Lipκ(A).
We now define a metric between measures that will be suitable for our goal.

2.2
Associated Gromov-Hausdorff-type topology
We now consider a Gromov-Hausdorff type topology defined using BLκ metric. The interested reader is
referred to [36] or [54] for a more general theory of Gromov-Hausdorff-type topologies. For later use, we
introduce terminologies.
• A measured metric space is a triplet (X, dX, µX) such that (X, dX) is a metric space, and µX ∈
M(X).
• A rooted metric space is a triplet (X, dX, ρX) such that (X, dX) is a metric space, and ρX ∈X is
a distinguished point called a root.
• A root-and-measured metric space is a quadruple (X, dX, ρX, µX) such that (X, dX, ρX) is a rooted
metric space, and µX ∈M(X).
• A root-preserving map is a map between two rooted metric spaces such that it maps the root of
the domain to that of the codomain.
• We say two measured metric spaces (X, dX, µX) and (Y, dY , µY ) are equivalent if and only if there
exists an isometry f : X →Y with µY = µX ◦f −1.
• We say two rooted metric spaces (X, dX, ρX) and (Y, dY , ρY ) are equivalent if and only if there
exists a root-preserving isometry f : X →Y .
• We say two root-and-measured metric spaces (X, dX, ρX, µX) and (Y, dY , ρY , µY ) are equivalent if
and only if there exists a root-preserving isometry f : X →Y with µY = µX ◦f −1.

2.3
Resistance forms and associated basic notions
This section is devoted to the definition of resistance forms and their basic properties. The interested
reader is referred to [40] for details on resistance forms. Although the term “resistance form” does not
explicitly appear, [48, Chapter 9] is also a valuable reference for the graph case.
Definition 2.33 (Resistance form, [40, Definition 3.1]). Let F be a non-empty set. A pair (E, F) is
called a resistance form if it satisfies the following conditions.
(RF1) F is a linear subspace of RF containing the constant functions. E is a non-negative symmetric
quadratic form on F satisfying
E(u, u) = 0 ⇐⇒u is a constant.
(RF2) Let ∼be an equivalence relation on F defined by u ∼v if and only if u −v is constant. Then
(F/ ∼, E) is a Hilbert space.
(RF3) If x ̸= y ∈F, then there exists u ∈F such that u(x) ̸= u(y).
(RF4) For any x, y ∈F,
R(x, y) := sup
|u(x) −u(y)|2
E(u, u)
: u ∈F, E(u, u) > 0

= inf{E(u, u) : u(x) = 1, u(y) = 0, u ∈F}−1
is finite.
(RF5) For u ∈F, set ¯u := 0 ∨(u ∧1). Then ¯u ∈F and E(¯u, ¯u) ≤E(u, u) hold.
The function R : F × F →[0, ∞) defined by (RF4) is a metric on F (see [38, Theorem 2.3.4]) and called
the resistance metric associated with (E, F).
More generally, we define an effective resistance R(A, B) between subsets A and B of F by setting
R(A, B) = inf{E(u, u) : u|A = 1, u|B = 0, u ∈F}−1,
inf ∅:= ∞.
We write R(x, B) for R({x}, B) and R(x, y) = R({x}, {y}).
Note that, for any u ∈F and x, y ∈F, it holds that
|u(x) −u(y)|2 ≤E(u, u)R(x, y)
(2.4)

Convergence rate estimates for semigroups and heat kernels associated with resistance forms
by (RF4). In particular, u ∈F is 1/2-Hölder continuous with respect to the resistance metric R, and
satisfies ∥u∥Lip1/2 ≤E(u, u)1/2.
For the following definition, recall the effective resistance on an electrical network with a finite vertex
set from [48, Section 9.4]. See also [38, Section 2.1] for the relation between Laplacians and resistance
forms on finite sets.
Definition 2.34 (Resistance metric, [38, Definition 2.3.2]). Let F be a set. A function R : F ×F →[0, ∞)
is called a resistance metric if and only if, for any non-empty finite set V ⊆F, there exists an electrical
network G with the vertex set V such that the effective resistance on G coincides with R|V ×V .
A resistance metric is indeed a metric on F (see [38, Theorem 2.3.4]). As explained by the following
theorem, the term “resistance metric” in the above definition is compatible with the term “resistance
metric” (associated with a resistance form) in Definition 2.33.
Theorem 2.35 ([38, Theorem 2.3.6]). Let R be a resistance metric on a non-empty set F in the sense
of Definition 2.34. Then there exists a unique resistance form (E, F) on F such that R = R(E,F), where
R(E,F) is a resistance metric associated with (E, F).
In what follows, we assume that a non-empty set F is equipped with a resistance form (E, F), and
the metric space (F, R) is complete, separable and locally compact, where R is the resistance metric
corresponding to (E, F).
Definition 2.36 (Regular resistance form, [40, Definition 6.2]). The resistance form (E, F) is called
regular if and only if F ∩Cc(F) is dense in Cc(F) with respect to the supremum norm. Here, we denote
the set of compactly supported continuous functions on F by Cc(F).
We next introduce related Dirichlet forms and stochastic processes. Henceforth, we assume the regular-
ity of a resistance form (E, F) on F. Note that, since all functions lie in F is continuous by (2.4), any com-
pact resistance metric space is regular. Let µ be a (positive) Radon measure on F such that suppµ = F,
and L2(F, µ) be the set of (equivalence classes of) Borel measurable functions f : F →[−∞, ∞] such
that
R
F f 2dµ < ∞. We denote an inner product on L2(F, µ) by (·, ·)L2(F,µ). Note that, since µ is of
full support, each continuous function on F can be naturally regarded as an element of L2(F, µ). Then
(L2(F, µ) ∩F, E1), E1(u, v) := E(u, v) + (u, v)L2(F,µ) is a Hilbert space (see [40, Lemma 9.2]). Define
D ⊆L2(F, µ) by setting D := F ∩Cc(F)
E1 ⊆L2(F, µ) ∩F. By virtue of the regularity of (E, F), we have
from [40, Theorem 9.4] that (E, D) is a regular Dirichlet form on L2(F, µ). (See [31, Chapter 1.1] for the
definition of regular Dirichlet forms.) Moreover, standard theory gives us the existence of an associated
Hunt process ((Xt)t≥0, (Px)x∈F ) (e.g. [31, Theorem 7.2.1]). Note that such a process is, in general, only
specified uniquely for starting points outside a set of zero capacity. However, in this setting, every point
has strictly positive capacity (see [40, Theorem 9.9]), and so the process is defined uniquely everywhere.
In the rest of this section, we fix a full support Radon measure µ on a regular resistance metric space
(F, R) and corresponding Hunt process (X = (Xt)t≥0, (Px)x∈F ). We denote the expectation with respect
to Px by Ex[·].
Definition 2.37 (Recurrence of resistance forms, [55, Definition 3.13]). Let (E, F) be a resistance form
on F and write R for the corresponding resistance metric. We say that (E, F) and R are recurrent if and
only if the following condition is satisfied:

3
Continuity of heat kernels and α-potential densities
To establish estimates for semigroups and heat kernels, it is necessary to bound |
R
fdµ −
R
fdµn| for
suitable functions f (using dBLκ(µ, µn)).
Typical examples of such f are functions in the image of
semigroups or resolvents. Therefore, it is important to provide sharp bounds for the BLκ norm of such
functions. To this end, this section is devoted to estimating the Hölder regularity of the heat kernel, its
time derivative, and the α-potential density. While interesting in themselves, these estimates are crucial,
as their optimality affect the exponents in our main result.
In this section, we fix an element (F, R, µ) ∈F◦(recall the definition of F◦from (1.4)), and the Hunt
process (X = (Xt)t≥0, (Px)x∈F ) corresponding to the triplet (F, R, µ). We denote the corresponding
resistance form, generator, semigroup, resolvent, heat kernel and α-potential density by (E, F), ∆, (Pt)t,
(Gα)α, p, and gα, respectively (recall the definition of these objects from the end of Section 2.3). We
abbreviate p(t, x, ·) to pt,x. For a non-empty open set U ⊊F and its complement A := F \ U, we also
write ∆U, pU, Gα
A, GA, gα
A, and gA for the generator, heat kernel, resolvent, Green operator, α-potential
density, and Green function associated with the killed process XU. If A is a singleton {x}, we abbreviate
the index {x} to x. For instance, we write gx for g{x}. Finally, we assume throughout this paper that
every metric space under consideration consists of at least two distinct points (except for Section 2.1).
In this section, we seek explicit formulas for the constants involved in the inequalities that we will
prove for the heat kernels and potential densities, specifically written in terms of the underlying space
data. Such dependence is of particular importance when considering convergence rate estimates in non-
compact settings. We denote constants appearing in lemma (or proposition/theorem) X by cX or c(n)
X ,
except for Proposition 3.2. Also, we write A ≲B if there exists a universal constant C such that A ≤CB.

Estimates for semigroups
The aim of this section is to transfer our previous estimates on the resolvent to corresponding ones on
the semigroup. Our main result is Theorem 5.1 below. We use the same notation and assumptions as
in Section 4. In particular, we assume that (Fn, Rn, µn) ∈F◦
c converges to (F, R, µ) ∈F◦

c5.7, which is defined below in the statement of Lemma 5.7. More generally, as in the previous section, we
denote constants appearing in proposition, lemma, or theorem X by cX or c(n)
X . Note that we assume that
any metric space contains at least two distinct points. Also, we write A ≲B if there exists a universal
constant C such that A ≤CB

In estimating the resolvent, roughly speaking, since we have an explicit formula to write Gα in terms
of Gx, it is enough to estimate |Gxf(y) −Gn
xnf(yn)|. This can be done by extending the domain of gx
in a natural way. On the other hand, the heat kernel does not have such a natural extension. Although
we have a McShane extension, it has a shortcoming that the operation of extension is not linear. To
overcome these obstacles, we introduce the following operator Jr
n.

6
Estimates for heat kernels
In this section, we derive the convergence rate for heat kernels by utilizing the corresponding estimates
for semigroups.
As a corollary, we also establish a semigroup estimate under assumption (A1).
See
Corollary 6.3 for more details. We use the same notation and assumptions as in Section 5. In particular,
we assume that (Fn, Rn, µn) ∈F◦
c converges to (F, R, µ) ∈F◦

definition of F◦

(M, d). Also, while we have pursued the constants to handle the non-compact cases, we do not do so in
this section. Thus we write A ≲B if there exists a time-independent constant (not necessarily universal)
C ∈(0, ∞) such that A ≤CB.
Recall the assumptions (A1), (A2), (A3) and parameters s0, s1, θ from Assumption 1.1. Then our
main results in this section are the following Theorem 6.1 and Corollary 6.3.

Remark 7.3. Suppose that µ is s-Ahlfors regular and let r > 0. Then one sufficient condition that
makes µ(r) s-Ahlfors regular is the following:
(C) There exists an ε > 0 such that for all x ∈F (r) and a ∈(0, diamF (r)], the set B(x, a) ∩B(ρ, r)
contains a ball with radius εa.
This requirement (C) is known as the corkscrew condition. We note that it is known that every A-uniform
domain (see [53, Definition 2.3] for the definition) satisfies the corkscrew condition with ε = 1/3A (see
[53, Lemma 3.4]). Therefore, one sufficient condition that the Assumption 2-a is satisfied is that there
exists a constant A such that B(ρ, r) is an A-uniform domain for r ≥r0.
Lemma 7.4.
(i) If (F, R) is uniformly perfect, then Assumption 2-a implies Assumption 2-b with θ = 1.
(ii) Under Assumption 2-b, for any r > 0, F (r) satisfies LRES(θ) with the same constant cLR.
(iii) Suppose that there exists a constant C ∈(0, ∞) such that Cr ≤diamF (r) for r > r0. If Assumptions
2-a and 2-b are satisfied with θ = 1, then Assumption 2-c also holds.
Proof of (i). This is an immediate consequence of Proposition 3.2(i).
Proof of (ii). Let (E, F) and (E(r), F(r)) be the resistance forms corresponding to (F, R), (F (r), R(r)).
Note that F(r) is given by F(r) := {u|F (r) : u ∈F} (see [40, Theorem 8.4]). We write h(r) : F(r) →F for

8
Examples
8.1
Real trees coded by functions
In this section, we provide an estimates for dτ,BLκ
K
(T f, T g) in terms of the supremum of f −g, where T f
is the real tree coded by a function f. Recall from the definition of dτ,BLκ
K
from Definition 2.23, and see
Definition 8.1 and succeeding paragraph for the precise definition of T f.
Real trees frequently arise in probability theory and remain a central topic of research. For more
background on random trees, the reader is referred to [47].
60


## Page 61


Convergence rate estimates for semigroups and heat kernels associated with resistance forms
Definition 8.1 (Real tree coded by a function, [1, Section 3]). Let f : [0, ∞) →[0, ∞) be a compactly
supported continuous function and set
σf = sup{t > 0 : f(t) > 0} < ∞.
Define a semi-metric ¯df on [0, σf] by setting
¯df(s, t) := f(t) + f(s) −2 inf
u∈[s,t] f(u),
0 ≤s ≤t ≤σf.
We define a real tree coded by f by
T f := [0, σf]/ ∼,
where s ∼t if and only if ¯df(s, t) = 0.
Let T f be the real tree coded by a function f ∈C+
c (R≥0), where
C+
c (R≥0) := {f : [0, ∞) →[0, ∞) : f is a compactly supported continuous function}.
We define a metric df on T f by
df(pf(s), pf(t)) := ¯df(s, t),
0 ≤s, t ≤σf,
where pf : [0, σf] →T f is the canonical projection.
Thus, T f is equipped with the structure of a
metric space. Note that since pf is continuous, (T f, df) is compact. Furthermore, we define a finite
Borel measure mf on T f as the pushforward of the Lebesgue measure on [0, σf] under pf. By abuse of
notation, we also refer to the triplet T f := (T f, df, mf) as the real tree coded by f. It is known that a
real tree coded by a function (T f, df) is a resistance metric space. See [37, Proposition 5.1] for its proof.
We now provide an estimate for dτ,BLκ
K
(T f, T g). Note that similar results are shown in [1, Proposition
3.3] and [56, Proposition 8.3].
Proposition 8.2. For f, g ∈C+
c (R≥0), we have
dτ,BLκ
K
(T f, T g) ≤2∥f −g∥[0,σf ∧σg] + 2κ(σf ∧σg)∥f −g∥κ
[0,σf ∧σg] + |σf −σg|,
where
∥f −g∥[0,σf ∧σg] :=
sup
u∈[0,σf ∧σg]
|f(u) −g(u)|.
Remark 8.3. In some cases, we let ρf := pf(0) be the root of T f, and define the real tree coded by f
as T f = (T f, df, mf, ρf). In this case, by using the distance dτ,BLκ
K•
defined in Definition 2.16, we obtain
the following:
dτ,BLκ
K

[0,σf ∧σg] + |σf −σg|.

R := {(xf, xg) ∈T f × T g : ∃t ∈[0, ∞), xf = pf(t), xg = pg(t)},
and
dis(R) := sup{|df(xf, yf) −dg(xg, yg)| : (xf, xg), (yf, yg) ∈R}.
The quantity dis(R) is called a distortion of R. Note that the canonical projections R →T f and R →T g
are surjection. Then we have
dis(R) ≤4∥f −g∥[0,σf ∧σg].

Remark 8.5. Previous studies [2, 24] have demonstrated that, for Galton-Watson trees T Sn whose
offspring distributions have finite or possibly infinite variance, the sequence of scaled contour functions Sn
converges in a suitable sense to twice the Brownian excursion 2W. Furthermore, convergence of random
walks on Tn to the natural stochastic process on T 2W has been established in [18], and weak convergence
of T Sn to T 2W in the Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov topology was demonstrated in [56, Corollary 8.7].
Consequently, as suggested by our main results and the arguments presented in this section, constructing
a suitable coupling between Sn and 2W would allow us to quantify the convergence obtained in [18].
8.2
Sierpinski gasket
In this section, we derive estimates for the semigroup and heat kernel on the Sierpinski gasket. The study
of Brownian motion on the Sierpinski gasket, including its rigorous construction, began about forty years
ago. In [10, 32, 45], this process was constructed as a scaling limit of random walks on approximating
finite graphs. Also, rate estimates for the generators and resolvents were demonstrated in [57]. Although
this approximation has been known for several decades, quantitative convergence rates for semigroups
and heat kernels have remained unknown. In Theorem 8.9, we establish explicit estimates for these rates.
To deal with the Sierpinski gasket, we begin slightly more general setting. Let fi : Rk →Rk, i =
1, . . . , n be similitudes (with respect to the Euclidean metric). Here, we say a function f : Rk →Rk is a
similitude or an r−similitude if there exists a constant r ∈(0, 1) such that |f(x) −f(y)| = r|x −y| for
any x, y ∈Rk. Then there exists a unique non-empty compact set K ⊆Rk satisfying K = ∪n
i=1fi(K),
and the set K is called the self-similar set with respect to (fi)n
i=1 (see [38, Theorem 1.1.4]). If we set
fi(x) = 1
2(x + pi), x ∈R2, i = 1, 2, 3 for vertices pi of a regular triangle, then K will be the Sierpinski
gasket. We return to the general setting. Suppose that each fi is an ri-similitude with respect to the
Euclidean metric dE. We say (fi)n
i=1 satisfies the open set condition if there exists a non-empty bounded
open set O ⊆Rk such that ∪n
i=1fi(O) ⊆O and fi(O) ∩fj(O) = ∅for i ̸= j. It is known that then the
Hausdorff dimension dimH K of K with respect to dE is given by a unique positive number α satisfying
Pn

confirm the regularity of the Hausdorff measure on K. For a proof of the following lemma, see a proof of
[34, Section 5.3 Theorem 1].
Lemma 8.6. Let (fi)n
i=1 be a family of similitudes, and α be the Hausdorff dimension of the self-similar
set K ⊆Rk with respect to dE.
If (fi)n

8.3
One dimensional Bouchaud trap model
In physics, aging refers to a phenomenon in which a system’s dynamics progressively slow down without
reaching equilibrium on laboratory time scales, so that its current state carries information about its age.
Such behavior is typical in disordered media such as spin glasses at low temperatures. Originally, the
model that is nowadays called the Bouchaud trap model (BTM), was introduced in [13] to understand
aging phenomena, and the one-dimensional version considered in this section was firstly studied in [30].
More mathematical details on the BTM can be found in [11].
To describe the (scaled) one-dimensional BTM precisely, we introduce a family of i.i.d.
random
variables (τx)x∈Z on a probability space (Ω, F, P).
For a given α > 0, we suppose that τx satisfies
P(τ0 ≥u) = u−α, u > 1. In this section, however, we focus on the regime where the mean trapping time
is finite, namely α > 1. The (scaled) dynamics of the BTM are given by an n−1Z-valued Markov process
Xn = (Xn
t ) whose generator is given by
∆nf(x/n) = n2

the value of τx increases, the process Xn is more likely to stay trapped in the state x/n for an extended
period. In terms of the resistance metric, this can be described as follows. Let F = R and Fn = n−1Z

Proof of Theorem 3.9
In this appendix, we give a proof of Theorem 3.9. Let (F, R, µ, ρ), (Fn, Rn, µn, ρn), A, An, and (M, dM)
be as in Theorem 3.9. We denote the processes corresponding to (F, R, µ) and (Fn, Rn, µn) by (X =
(Xt)t, (Px)x∈F ) and (Xn = (Xn
t )t, (P n
x )x∈Fn), respectively.
We regard the hitting time of A ⊆M as a function on D := D(R≥0, M) := {x : R≥0 →M :
x is càdlàg.}. Precisely, we define σA : D →[0, ∞] for A ⊆M by setting
σA(x) = inf{t > 0 : x(t) ∈A},
x ∈D.
We equip D with the J1 topology. We refer the reader to [27, Chapter 3] for details on the J1 topology.
We denote the set of the continuity points of x ∈D by Cx.
Lemma B.1. For any open subset U ⊆M, σU is upper semicontinuous. In particular, it is measurable.
Proof. It suffices to show that lim supn→∞σU(xn) ≤σU(x) for any convergent sequence (xn) ⊆D and
its limit x ∈D. Thus, we may assume that σU(x) < ∞. Fix an arbitrary ε > 0. Then we can find
t0 ∈[τU(x), τU(x) + ε) such that x(t0) ∈U. Since U is open and x lies in D, we can verify that for any
sufficiently large j ∈N, there exists tj ∈[t0, t0 +1/j]∩Cx such that x(tj) ∈U. The J1 convergence yields
that limn→∞xn(tj) = x(tj) ∈U for each j, since tj is in Cx. Therefore, for each j, there exists Nj ∈N
such that xn(tj) ∈U whenever n > Nj. This implies that lim supn→∞σU(xn) ≤tj for any j. Letting
j →∞and then ε →0, we obtain the result.
Note that since the space is recurrent, Pρ(σA(X) < ∞) = 1 by [17, Theorem 5.2.16(i)] and [31,
Theorem 4.7.1(iii)].
Lemma B.2. Let Uk := ∪a∈AB(a, 1/k). Then σUk(X) converges to σA(X) almost surely with respect to
Pρ.
Proof. Since σUk(X) is increasing in k, it has a pointwise limit σ∞. By σUk(X) ≤σA(X), we have
σ∞≤σA(X) < ∞Pρ-a.s. The quasi-left-continuity of X (see [31, (A.2.4)] for the definition of quasi-left-
continuity) yields that
Pρ

= Pρ (σ∞< ∞) = 1.
Since XσUk (X) ∈Uk, and A is closed, we deduce σ∞≥σA(X) Pρ-a.s.
Now we can prove the result in the compact case.
